People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc... I mean, I ain't really sure where to start, to be honest with you. It's just... I guess the main thing, what really brings me in today, is I just hardly eat right anymore. I don't know why, it's like the hunger just packed up and left, or I just forget, or... well, like, take yesterday for example. Or maybe it was Tuesday? Yeah, Tuesday. I woke up, made a pot of coffee, and I just drank that. Didn't even think about food until way, way later in the afternoon. 

And even then, all I could stomach was maybe a piece of bread. Just a plain piece of white bread, didn't even put anything on it. And that's been happening a whole lot lately. Most days, honestly... some days it is just coffee and maybe a piece of bread for the whole entire day. It's getting ridiculous because I used to eat regular, you know? I always ate fine before. But now, I had to take a pocket knife and punch a brand new hole in my belt just to keep my jeans from sliding down my waist. My pants are just hanging loose on me, like they belong to somebody else entirely, and I just... I can't seem to force myself to eat a real meal, and it's just going on and on like this, day after day.

Well, if I really sit back and reckon on it... this whole spell, this dark cloud or whatever you want to call it, it's been hanging over me for about five months now. Yeah, right around five months. Five months of just wanting to hide away and watching the sun come up and go down, just waiting for the day to be over, and then the next day it starts all over again. I just don't understand how something like this can just grab hold of a person for five solid months and not ever let go, not even for a minute to let you catch your breath. It just goes on and on, and I keep thinking maybe tomorrow it'll lift, but it never does. Five months is a long time to feel like you're carrying around a heavy, hollowed-out chest, doc. I just sit there with that heavy, empty chest, wanting to keep my head down so the world just goes on and leaves me be. It's just this awful, dark thing right from the second I open my eyes, and it just stays on me, making me not want to do a single thing but stay out of sight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heavy feeling in your chest and not wanting to do anything?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and reckon on it... this whole spell, this dark cloud or whatever you want to call it, it's been hanging over me for about five months now. I just stayed inside. And ever since then, for five whole months, it ain't let up once. I just don't understand how something like this can just grab hold of a person for five solid months and not ever let go, not even for a minute to let you catch your breath. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now that you ask about feeling like that... I mean, there was this one time that sticks out in my head. It was right after I got let go from my last job. Fired, I mean. They just up and told me they didn't need me around no more, and you'd think a man would be real torn up about losing his paycheck, right? Specially with how hard things can get. But I didn't feel bad at all. Actually, it was the exact opposite, which is strange to think on now. I walked out of that place and I just felt... I don't know, I just felt fired up. Completely fired up, like my blood was running hot and fast and I couldn't even stand still. 

I felt kind of untouchable, to be honest with you. Like they did me a massive favor and I was about to just take on the whole entire world. I had all this energy burning up inside me, pacing around, doing this and that, thinking about all these big things I was gonna get into now that I wasn't tied down to that place. I felt like a million bucks. 

But the thing of it was, even though I felt so good and so untouchable, I had a real short fuse with folks. Like, a hair-trigger. If anybody—like my buddy, or just folks I'd run into—if they looked at me pacing around and said something like, "Hey man, you need to calm down," or "Maybe you should just slow down a second," I would just snap at 'em. I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It just goes on and on, and I keep thinking maybe tomorrow it'll lift, but it never does. Five months is a long time to feel like you're carrying around a heavy, hollowed-out chest, doc. A real long time to just be sitting there letting the world pass you by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or so good that you didn't even need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about feeling like that... I mean, there was this one time that sticks out in my head. It was right after I got let go from my last job. Fired, I mean. They just up and told me they didn't need me around no more, and you'd think a man would be real torn up about losing his paycheck, right? Specially with how hard things can get. But I didn't feel bad at all. Actually, it was the exact opposite, which is strange to think on now. I walked out of that place and I just felt... I don't know, I just felt fired up. Completely fired up, like my blood was running hot and fast and I couldn't even stand still. \n\nI felt kind of untouchable, to be honest with you. Like they did me a massive favor and I was about to just take on the whole entire world. I had all this energy burning up inside me, pacing around, doing this and that, thinking about all these big things I was gonna get into now that I wasn't tied down to that place. I felt like a million bucks. \n\nBut the thing of it was, even though I felt so good and so untouchable, I had a real short fuse with folks. Like, a hair-trigger. If anybody—like my buddy, or just folks I'd run into—if they looked at me pacing around and said something like, \"Hey man, you need to calm down,\" or \"Maybe you should just slow down a second,\" I would just snap at 'em. I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It was just this wild, fired-up feeling that I couldn't turn off, and I sure didn't want nobody trying to turn it off for me neither.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long did you feel that way after you lost your job—how many days or weeks did that high and energetic feeling last?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"The scary swing from feeling driven after I was fired to being this low has been going on for about a month.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's hard to pin down the exact number of days sometimes because when you're moving that fast, time just sort of blurs together. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. 

Yeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.

I know because I remember it was right around the time I was supposed to help a buddy of mine clear out some junk behind his property, and I just couldn't make myself walk over there. I just stayed inside. And ever since then, for five whole months, it ain't let up once. Not for a single afternoon. \n\nYou'd think a man might get a break, you know, maybe wake up one morning and that cinderblock wouldn't be sitting there on his chest, but no. A hundred and fifty days, give or take, of just waking up and feeling like I'm already beat down before my feet even hit the floorboards. Five months of just wanting to hide away and watching the sun come up and go down, just waiting for the day to be over, and then the next day it starts all over again. I just don't understand how something like this can just grab hold of a person for five solid months and not ever let go, not even for a minute to let you catch your breath. It just goes on and on, and I keep thinking maybe tomorrow it'll lift, but it never does. Five months is a long time to feel like you're carrying around a heavy, hollowed-out chest, doc. A real long time to just be sitting there letting the world pass you by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or so good that you didn't even need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about feeling like that... I mean, there was this one time that sticks out in my head. It was right after I got let go from my last job. Fired, I mean. They just up and told me they didn't need me around no more, and you'd think a man would be real torn up about losing his paycheck, right? Specially with how hard things can get. But I didn't feel bad at all. Actually, it was the exact opposite, which is strange to think on now. I walked out of that place and I just felt... I don't know, I just felt fired up. Completely fired up, like my blood was running hot and fast and I couldn't even stand still. \n\nI felt kind of untouchable, to be honest with you. Like they did me a massive favor and I was about to just take on the whole entire world. I had all this energy burning up inside me, pacing around, doing this and that, thinking about all these big things I was gonna get into now that I wasn't tied down to that place. I felt like a million bucks. \n\nBut the thing of it was, even though I felt so good and so untouchable, I had a real short fuse with folks. Like, a hair-trigger. If anybody—like my buddy, or just folks I'd run into—if they looked at me pacing around and said something like, \"Hey man, you need to calm down,\" or \"Maybe you should just slow down a second,\" I would just snap at 'em. I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It was just this wild, fired-up feeling that I couldn't turn off, and I sure didn't want nobody trying to turn it off for me neither.\n\nDoctor: How long did you feel that way after you lost your job—how many days or weeks did that high and energetic feeling last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to pin down the exact number of days sometimes because when you're moving that fast, time just sort of blurs together. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. \n\nYeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had more than one period in your life where you felt extremely high, energetic, or didn't need much sleep?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I really sit back and chew on that for a minute... I mean, I'm thirty-five years old, right? You'd think if a man was prone to getting all fired up like a roman candle, it would've happened a few times by now. But honestly, looking back over my whole life, all the way back to when I was just a kid running around, or in my twenties trying to figure things out... no. I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. 

I mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. 

I keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. So no, I haven't had any other times like that. It was just that one stretch right up until I crashed down into this hole. Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.

It just goes on and on, and I keep thinking maybe tomorrow it'll lift, but it never does. Five months is a long time to feel like you're carrying around a heavy, hollowed-out chest, doc. A real long time to just be sitting there letting the world pass you by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or so good that you didn't even need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about feeling like that... I mean, there was this one time that sticks out in my head. It was right after I got let go from my last job. Fired, I mean. They just up and told me they didn't need me around no more, and you'd think a man would be real torn up about losing his paycheck, right? Specially with how hard things can get. But I didn't feel bad at all. Actually, it was the exact opposite, which is strange to think on now. I walked out of that place and I just felt... I don't know, I just felt fired up. Completely fired up, like my blood was running hot and fast and I couldn't even stand still. \n\nI felt kind of untouchable, to be honest with you. Like they did me a massive favor and I was about to just take on the whole entire world. I had all this energy burning up inside me, pacing around, doing this and that, thinking about all these big things I was gonna get into now that I wasn't tied down to that place. I felt like a million bucks. \n\nBut the thing of it was, even though I felt so good and so untouchable, I had a real short fuse with folks. Like, a hair-trigger. If anybody—like my buddy, or just folks I'd run into—if they looked at me pacing around and said something like, \"Hey man, you need to calm down,\" or \"Maybe you should just slow down a second,\" I would just snap at 'em. I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It was just this wild, fired-up feeling that I couldn't turn off, and I sure didn't want nobody trying to turn it off for me neither.\n\nDoctor: How long did you feel that way after you lost your job—how many days or weeks did that high and energetic feeling last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to pin down the exact number of days sometimes because when you're moving that fast, time just sort of blurs together. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. \n\nYeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had more than one period in your life where you felt extremely high, energetic, or didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and chew on that for a minute... I mean, I'm thirty-five years old, right? You'd think if a man was prone to getting all fired up like a roman candle, it would've happened a few times by now. But honestly, looking back over my whole life, all the way back to when I was just a kid running around, or in my twenties trying to figure things out... no. I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. \n\nI mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. \n\nI keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. So no, I haven't had any other times like that. It was just that one stretch right up until I crashed down into this hole. Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now that you're asking about feeling low before this... I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. 

Sure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. 

And if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. 

I keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. You'd get mad, you'd mope, and then the next day you'd be out throwing a football around. It never stuck to me like this. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. It's all just hitting me for the very first time.

I woke up, made a pot of coffee, and I just drank that. Didn't even think about food until way, way later in the afternoon. \n\nAnd even then, all I could stomach was maybe a piece of bread. Most days, honestly... some days it is just coffee and maybe a piece of bread for the whole entire day. It's getting ridiculous because I used to eat regular, you know? I always ate fine before. But now, I had to take a pocket knife and punch a brand new hole in my belt just to keep my jeans from sliding down my waist. My pants are just hanging loose on me, like they belong to somebody else entirely, and I just... I can't seem to force myself to eat a real meal, and it's just going on and on like this, day after day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities lately?\n\nPatient: Well, as for my mood... man, it's just bad. I don't even know the right words for it, really, but it's just... well, most days, I wake up, and before I even swing my legs over the side of the mattress, I get this heavy feeling. Just this real heavy, completely empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's like a cinderblock is sitting on me, but it's hollowed out inside, if that makes any sense at all. \n\nAnd I just lie there staring up at the ceiling, thinking to myself that there ain't nothing good waiting for me out there. Not one single good thing is gonna happen out in the world, so what's the point of even trying to be a part of it? I just get this overwhelming urge to keep my head down. You know, just hide away from everything and everybody. I used to like doing things, fixing things up around the place, but now I don't have a lick of interest in any of that. I just sit there with that heavy, empty chest, wanting to keep my head down so the world just goes on and leaves me be. It's just this awful, dark thing right from the second I open my eyes, and it just stays on me, making me not want to do a single thing but stay out of sight.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heavy feeling in your chest and not wanting to do anything?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and reckon on it... this whole spell, this dark cloud or whatever you want to call it, it's been hanging over me for about five months now. Yeah, right around five months. I know because I remember it was right around the time I was supposed to help a buddy of mine clear out some junk behind his property, and I just couldn't make myself walk over there. I just stayed inside. And ever since then, for five whole months, it ain't let up once. Not for a single afternoon. \n\nYou'd think a man might get a break, you know, maybe wake up one morning and that cinderblock wouldn't be sitting there on his chest, but no. A hundred and fifty days, give or take, of just waking up and feeling like I'm already beat down before my feet even hit the floorboards. Five months of just wanting to hide away and watching the sun come up and go down, just waiting for the day to be over, and then the next day it starts all over again. I just don't understand how something like this can just grab hold of a person for five solid months and not ever let go, not even for a minute to let you catch your breath. It just goes on and on, and I keep thinking maybe tomorrow it'll lift, but it never does. Five months is a long time to feel like you're carrying around a heavy, hollowed-out chest, doc. A real long time to just be sitting there letting the world pass you by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or so good that you didn't even need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about feeling like that... I mean, there was this one time that sticks out in my head. It was right after I got let go from my last job. Fired, I mean. They just up and told me they didn't need me around no more, and you'd think a man would be real torn up about losing his paycheck, right? Specially with how hard things can get. But I didn't feel bad at all. Actually, it was the exact opposite, which is strange to think on now. I walked out of that place and I just felt... I don't know, I just felt fired up. Completely fired up, like my blood was running hot and fast and I couldn't even stand still. \n\nI felt kind of untouchable, to be honest with you. Like they did me a massive favor and I was about to just take on the whole entire world. I had all this energy burning up inside me, pacing around, doing this and that, thinking about all these big things I was gonna get into now that I wasn't tied down to that place. I felt like a million bucks. \n\nBut the thing of it was, even though I felt so good and so untouchable, I had a real short fuse with folks. Like, a hair-trigger. If anybody—like my buddy, or just folks I'd run into—if they looked at me pacing around and said something like, \"Hey man, you need to calm down,\" or \"Maybe you should just slow down a second,\" I would just snap at 'em. I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It was just this wild, fired-up feeling that I couldn't turn off, and I sure didn't want nobody trying to turn it off for me neither.\n\nDoctor: How long did you feel that way after you lost your job—how many days or weeks did that high and energetic feeling last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to pin down the exact number of days sometimes because when you're moving that fast, time just sort of blurs together. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. \n\nYeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had more than one period in your life where you felt extremely high, energetic, or didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and chew on that for a minute... I mean, I'm thirty-five years old, right? You'd think if a man was prone to getting all fired up like a roman candle, it would've happened a few times by now. But honestly, looking back over my whole life, all the way back to when I was just a kid running around, or in my twenties trying to figure things out... no. I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. \n\nI mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. \n\nI keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. So no, I haven't had any other times like that. It was just that one stretch right up until I crashed down into this hole. Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent period of feeling down and losing interest in things, have you ever had any other times in your life where you felt persistently low, hopeless, or uninterested in activities for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you're asking about feeling low before this... I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. \n\nSure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. \n\nAnd if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. \n\nI keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. You'd get mad, you'd mope, and then the next day you'd be out throwing a football around. It never stuck to me like this. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. It's all just hitting me for the very first time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. \n\nYeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had more than one period in your life where you felt extremely high, energetic, or didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and chew on that for a minute... I mean, I'm thirty-five years old, right? You'd think if a man was prone to getting all fired up like a roman candle, it would've happened a few times by now. But honestly, looking back over my whole life, all the way back to when I was just a kid running around, or in my twenties trying to figure things out... no. I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. \n\nI mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. \n\nI keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. \n\nSure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. \n\nAnd if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. \n\nI keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, like I was just saying a minute ago... no. Never before this. I know you're trying to make sure, but I swear on my life, before this whole mess started, I never had a spell like this in my whole thirty-five years. Never two weeks, never nothing. 

But this one right now... man, since you're asking about feeling down and hopeless, let me tell you exactly what it's doing to me right now, because it's just eating me alive. Most days, I mean practically every single morning since I crashed down from that high, I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I got this... this heavy, empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's like a physical weight, you know? Like somebody poured lead into my ribs while I was sleeping. 

And my brain just starts telling me right then and there that there ain't no point in moving. It's just this overwhelming sense that nothing good is waiting for me out there. Not a single good thing. No job to go to, no reason to even pull the covers off. So I just want to keep my head down. Literally, just keep my head down on the pillow, pull the blanket up over my ears, and hide. Because if there ain't nothing good waiting for me, why should I even try to face the day? I just lie there with that heavy emptiness, thinking about how I used to be able to just get up and go, and now it takes every ounce of whatever I got left just to breathe in and out. It's a miserable way to wake up, doc, just already defeated, keeping my head down so the world just leaves me alone.

Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent period of feeling down and losing interest in things, have you ever had any other times in your life where you felt persistently low, hopeless, or uninterested in activities for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you're asking about feeling low before this... I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. \n\nSure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. \n\nAnd if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. \n\nI keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. You'd get mad, you'd mope, and then the next day you'd be out throwing a football around. It never stuck to me like this. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. It's all just hitting me for the very first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other episodes in your life where you felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things for two weeks or more, before this most recent episode?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, like I was just saying a minute ago... no. Never before this. I know you're trying to make sure, but I swear on my life, before this whole mess started, I never had a spell like this in my whole thirty-five years. Never two weeks, never nothing. \n\nBut this one right now... man, since you're asking about feeling down and hopeless, let me tell you exactly what it's doing to me right now, because it's just eating me alive. Most days, I mean practically every single morning since I crashed down from that high, I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I got this... this heavy, empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's like a physical weight, you know? Like somebody poured lead into my ribs while I was sleeping. \n\nAnd my brain just starts telling me right then and there that there ain't no point in moving. It's just this overwhelming sense that nothing good is waiting for me out there. Not a single good thing. No job to go to, no reason to even pull the covers off. So I just want to keep my head down. Literally, just keep my head down on the pillow, pull the blanket up over my ears, and hide. Because if there ain't nothing good waiting for me, why should I even try to face the day? I just lie there with that heavy emptiness, thinking about how I used to be able to just get up and go, and now it takes every ounce of whatever I got left just to breathe in and out. It's a miserable way to wake up, doc, just already defeated, keeping my head down so the world just leaves me alone.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince you mentioned this current episode is the first time you’ve felt this level of hopelessness and lack of interest, have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life—before this most recent one—where you felt persistently down or uninterested in things for a week or more?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to like walking by the lake and working on my little model kits, but now they just sit there and I look at them like they belong to somebody else.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I know you gotta ask these things every which way, checking if it was two weeks, or a week, or whatever number of days... but I'm telling you straight, no. Nothing like that ever happened to me before this whole nightmare started. Not for a week, not even for a few days. I was just... I was just a regular guy who liked his life, you know? 

But this thing right now, this lack of interest you just mentioned... man, it's just stripping every last thing away from me. It's like... well, take the stuff I used to do to pass the time. I used to like walking by the lake. Just getting out there in the air, smelling the water, maybe watching the birds or skipping a rock or two just to clear my head. It was nice. And I had these little model kits I'd work on, too. Cars, mostly, you know, old muscle cars. I'd sit at the kitchen table for hours with the little tubes of glue and the tiny decals, getting the engine pieces just right. It gave me something to do with my hands, something to actually focus on and be proud of when I finished painting it. 

But now... I don't know. Now they just sit there. The boxes are just sitting there gathering dust on the table, and I look at them like they belong to somebody else. Like some other guy bought 'em and left 'em in my house. I don't feel a single pull to go out to that lake, either. It's just water. It's just plastic pieces on a table. I don't care about none of it anymore. It's like the part of me that used to actually like doing things, the part that could find a little joy in a quiet afternoon... it just dried up and blew away, and I can't figure out how to get it back.

I mean, I'd snap at 'em real fast. I'd bite their head right off before they could even finish their sentence. I just couldn't stand anybody telling me to slow down because I felt like I was finally moving at the right speed, you know? Like I was ten steps ahead of everybody else and they were all just dragging their feet in the mud. So yeah, I felt real good, real energized, but man, I was snapping at people left and right if they tried to put the brakes on me. It was just this wild, fired-up feeling that I couldn't turn off, and I sure didn't want nobody trying to turn it off for me neither.\n\nDoctor: How long did you feel that way after you lost your job—how many days or weeks did that high and energetic feeling last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to pin down the exact number of days sometimes because when you're moving that fast, time just sort of blurs together. But if I really trace it back... I got fired, right? And then I had that whole spell where I was just on fire, couldn't be stopped, snapping at folks who told me to slow down. That whole wild ride... and then the crash that came after it, landing me in this heavy, empty place I was telling you about... well, that whole scary swing from feeling so driven and untouchable to being this low, it's been going on for about a month now. \n\nYeah, about a month. Because I remember looking at the calendar when they let me go, and it was right around the start of the month, and now here we are. It's just terrifying, doc, to think about how a man can go from feeling like he's ten steps ahead of the whole world, ready to take on anything, to just... nothing. Just a cinderblock on the chest. And it all happened in the span of a month. Thirty days of just swinging from the absolute highest high I've ever felt, where my blood was just humming, right down to this pitch-black hole where I can't even get out of bed. It's a month that feels like a whole year, to be honest. Just a solid month of my brain playing these awful tricks on me, throwing me up in the air and then slamming me down onto the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had more than one period in your life where you felt extremely high, energetic, or didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and chew on that for a minute... I mean, I'm thirty-five years old, right? You'd think if a man was prone to getting all fired up like a roman candle, it would've happened a few times by now. But honestly, looking back over my whole life, all the way back to when I was just a kid running around, or in my twenties trying to figure things out... no. I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. \n\nI mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. \n\nI keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. So no, I haven't had any other times like that. It was just that one stretch right up until I crashed down into this hole. Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent period of feeling down and losing interest in things, have you ever had any other times in your life where you felt persistently low, hopeless, or uninterested in activities for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you're asking about feeling low before this... I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. \n\nSure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. \n\nAnd if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. \n\nI keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. You'd get mad, you'd mope, and then the next day you'd be out throwing a football around. It never stuck to me like this. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. It's all just hitting me for the very first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other episodes in your life where you felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things for two weeks or more, before this most recent episode?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, like I was just saying a minute ago... no. Never before this. I know you're trying to make sure, but I swear on my life, before this whole mess started, I never had a spell like this in my whole thirty-five years. Never two weeks, never nothing. \n\nBut this one right now... man, since you're asking about feeling down and hopeless, let me tell you exactly what it's doing to me right now, because it's just eating me alive. Most days, I mean practically every single morning since I crashed down from that high, I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I got this... this heavy, empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's like a physical weight, you know? Like somebody poured lead into my ribs while I was sleeping. \n\nAnd my brain just starts telling me right then and there that there ain't no point in moving. It's just this overwhelming sense that nothing good is waiting for me out there. Not a single good thing. No job to go to, no reason to even pull the covers off. So I just want to keep my head down. Literally, just keep my head down on the pillow, pull the blanket up over my ears, and hide. Because if there ain't nothing good waiting for me, why should I even try to face the day? I just lie there with that heavy emptiness, thinking about how I used to be able to just get up and go, and now it takes every ounce of whatever I got left just to breathe in and out. It's a miserable way to wake up, doc, just already defeated, keeping my head down so the world just leaves me alone.\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned this current episode is the first time you’ve felt this level of hopelessness and lack of interest, have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life—before this most recent one—where you felt persistently down or uninterested in things for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Doc, I know you gotta ask these things every which way, checking if it was two weeks, or a week, or whatever number of days... but I'm telling you straight, no. Nothing like that ever happened to me before this whole nightmare started. Not for a week, not even for a few days. I was just... I was just a regular guy who liked his life, you know? \n\nBut this thing right now, this lack of interest you just mentioned... man, it's just stripping every last thing away from me. It's like... well, take the stuff I used to do to pass the time. I used to like walking by the lake. Just getting out there in the air, smelling the water, maybe watching the birds or skipping a rock or two just to clear my head. It was nice. And I had these little model kits I'd work on, too. Cars, mostly, you know, old muscle cars. I'd sit at the kitchen table for hours with the little tubes of glue and the tiny decals, getting the engine pieces just right. It gave me something to do with my hands, something to actually focus on and be proud of when I finished painting it. \n\nBut now... I don't know. Now they just sit there. The boxes are just sitting there gathering dust on the table, and I look at them like they belong to somebody else. Like some other guy bought 'em and left 'em in my house. I don't feel a single pull to go out to that lake, either. It's just water. It's just plastic pieces on a table. I don't care about none of it anymore. It's like the part of me that used to actually like doing things, the part that could find a little joy in a quiet afternoon... it just dried up and blew away, and I can't figure out how to get it back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, doc... no, nothing like that. It was just that one time. I mean, I keep playing it over and over in my head, trying to figure out what happened to me, but it was just that one single stretch right after I lost my job. You know, I got the news, and instead of getting knocked down, I just got... wired. Like somebody plugged me straight into a wall socket. I was going a hundred miles an hour, didn't need to sleep, felt like I could take on the whole world. 

But since that crashed? Since that wore off? Absolutely not. I ain't felt a single drop of that energy since. Honestly, I mean, look at me now. I can barely keep my head up half the time, let alone feel "unusually good" or whatever. It's like that one spell just burned up every last ounce of fuel I had in my tank, and I've been running on empty ever since. 

I haven't had another burst like that, not even for a minute. No pacing around at night, no feeling like I'm on top of things, no skipping sleep. I wish I had even a fraction of that energy back just so I could stand up straight and maybe put those model cars together, but it just ain't there. It just happened that one time, and then... boom. Dropped straight down into this hole. So, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a second time. Just the one weird spell, and then this endless heavy feeling I've been telling you about. It hasn't come back at all.

Literally, just keep my head down on the pillow, pull the blanket up over my ears, and hide. Because if there ain't nothing good waiting for me, why should I even try to face the day? I can't rightly say I've ever felt anything quite like that one time after I got fired. \n\nI mean, sure, everybody gets a little happy now and then, like if you find a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk or maybe you have a good afternoon with your buddies, but that ain't what you're asking about. You're asking about that untouchable, fast-blood feeling where you don't even need to shut your eyes at night. And I gotta tell you, doc, if I had felt that before, I'd remember it. It's just not the kind of thing a person forgets, you know? It was so out of the blue, so strange for me to be buzzing like that, snapping at folks and feeling ten foot tall. \n\nI keep racking my brain while I'm sitting here, trying to think if maybe there was another time, maybe years ago, but there just ain't. It was just that one single time. Before that, I was just... well, just regular old me, taking things one day at a time, never moving that fast or feeling that wild. So no, I haven't had any other times like that. It was just that one stretch right up until I crashed down into this hole. Just the once, and that was more than enough to turn my whole world upside down, I'll tell you that much.\n\nDoctor: Before this most recent period of feeling down and losing interest in things, have you ever had any other times in your life where you felt persistently low, hopeless, or uninterested in activities for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you're asking about feeling low before this... I mean, I really gotta think back on that. You know, it's funny how your mind works, how it just kind of glosses over things until someone sits you down in a chair and asks you straight out. But if I really, really dig back into it... no, I don't think so. I mean, I can't recall a time where I felt this heavy. \n\nSure, I've had bad days. Everybody has bad days, right? Like, maybe you get a flat tire in the rain, or you don't have enough scratch to pay a bill, and you feel kind of bummed out for a day or two. You sit on the porch and just feel a little sorry for yourself. But that's just life, ain't it? That ain't what you're talking about. You're talking about this... this cinderblock feeling. This two weeks or more of just staring at the wall, feeling like there ain't no point to putting your boots on in the morning. \n\nAnd if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, and I'm trying to be as honest as I can be here, I've never felt anything like this current hole I'm in. This is brand new territory for me, and it's terrifying, to be frank. Before all this happened, before the job let me go and I had that wild, fired-up spell, I was just a regular guy. I'd get up, go to work, come home, maybe watch a little TV or have a beer. I never had these long stretches where I just couldn't care less about breathing, let alone doing anything else. \n\nI keep trying to remember if maybe when I was younger, you know, when you're a teenager and everything feels like the end of the world... but even then, it was just a passing thing. You'd get mad, you'd mope, and then the next day you'd be out throwing a football around. It never stuck to me like this. This thing I've got going on right now, this absolute nothingness where I don't even want to eat or talk to nobody... this is the first time I've ever been dragged down this far, and for this long. It's just this one time, right after that high feeling crashed. So, no, I haven't ever had a two-week stretch of feeling this hopeless before now. It's all just hitting me for the very first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other episodes in your life where you felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things for two weeks or more, before this most recent episode?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, like I was just saying a minute ago... no. Never before this. I know you're trying to make sure, but I swear on my life, before this whole mess started, I never had a spell like this in my whole thirty-five years. Never two weeks, never nothing. \n\nBut this one right now... man, since you're asking about feeling down and hopeless, let me tell you exactly what it's doing to me right now, because it's just eating me alive. Most days, I mean practically every single morning since I crashed down from that high, I wake up and before I even open my eyes, I got this... this heavy, empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's like a physical weight, you know? Like somebody poured lead into my ribs while I was sleeping. \n\nAnd my brain just starts telling me right then and there that there ain't no point in moving. It's just this overwhelming sense that nothing good is waiting for me out there. Not a single good thing. No job to go to, no reason to even pull the covers off. So I just want to keep my head down. Literally, just keep my head down on the pillow, pull the blanket up over my ears, and hide. Because if there ain't nothing good waiting for me, why should I even try to face the day? I just lie there with that heavy emptiness, thinking about how I used to be able to just get up and go, and now it takes every ounce of whatever I got left just to breathe in and out. It's a miserable way to wake up, doc, just already defeated, keeping my head down so the world just leaves me alone.\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned this current episode is the first time you’ve felt this level of hopelessness and lack of interest, have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life—before this most recent one—where you felt persistently down or uninterested in things for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Doc, I know you gotta ask these things every which way, checking if it was two weeks, or a week, or whatever number of days... but I'm telling you straight, no. Nothing like that ever happened to me before this whole nightmare started. Not for a week, not even for a few days. I was just... I was just a regular guy who liked his life, you know? \n\nBut this thing right now, this lack of interest you just mentioned... man, it's just stripping every last thing away from me. It's like... well, take the stuff I used to do to pass the time. I used to like walking by the lake. Just getting out there in the air, smelling the water, maybe watching the birds or skipping a rock or two just to clear my head. It was nice. And I had these little model kits I'd work on, too. Cars, mostly, you know, old muscle cars. I'd sit at the kitchen table for hours with the little tubes of glue and the tiny decals, getting the engine pieces just right. It gave me something to do with my hands, something to actually focus on and be proud of when I finished painting it. \n\nBut now... I don't know. Now they just sit there. The boxes are just sitting there gathering dust on the table, and I look at them like they belong to somebody else. Like some other guy bought 'em and left 'em in my house. I don't feel a single pull to go out to that lake, either. It's just water. It's just plastic pieces on a table. I don't care about none of it anymore. It's like the part of me that used to actually like doing things, the part that could find a little joy in a quiet afternoon... it just dried up and blew away, and I can't figure out how to get it back.\n\nDoctor: Since the time after you lost your job and felt that high, energetic state, have you had any other episodes where you felt similarly unusually good, energetic, or didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: No, doc... no, nothing like that. It was just that one time. I mean, I keep playing it over and over in my head, trying to figure out what happened to me, but it was just that one single stretch right after I lost my job. You know, I got the news, and instead of getting knocked down, I just got... wired. Like somebody plugged me straight into a wall socket. I was going a hundred miles an hour, didn't need to sleep, felt like I could take on the whole world. \n\nBut since that crashed? Since that wore off? Absolutely not. I ain't felt a single drop of that energy since. Honestly, I mean, look at me now. I can barely keep my head up half the time, let alone feel \"unusually good\" or whatever. It's like that one spell just burned up every last ounce of fuel I had in my tank, and I've been running on empty ever since. \n\nI haven't had another burst like that, not even for a minute. No pacing around at night, no feeling like I'm on top of things, no skipping sleep. I wish I had even a fraction of that energy back just so I could stand up straight and maybe put those model cars together, but it just ain't there. It just happened that one time, and then... boom. Dropped straight down into this hole. So, no, to answer your question, there hasn't been a second time. Just the one weird spell, and then this endless heavy feeling I've been telling you about. It hasn't come back at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doc, I feel like we're going round in circles here, but I get it, you gotta be thorough. But like I just said, and like I said before... no. Never. Not for a few days, not for a week, not for a single afternoon. 

Before that one time right after I got let go from the plant, I never felt anything like that. I was just... normal. I mean, sure, maybe I'd have a good day if the weather was nice or if I found a twenty-dollar bill on the sidewalk, but nothing like that wired, buzzing feeling. I always needed my sleep, you know? Seven, eight hours a night, regular as clockwork. My old man used to say I could sleep through a freight train driving through the living room. 

So, no, I never had a time where I didn't need to sleep, or where I felt like I was running on rocket fuel. It was just that one single stretch, right after the job fell through. And honestly, I wish I could say there was another time, maybe before all this, where I felt that good without the crash, but there just wasn't. My whole life, up until a few months ago, was just... steady. Wake up, go to work, come home, work on my models, go to sleep. Nothing extreme. No zooming around, no feeling like a superhero. Just regular life. And now... well, now I don't even have that. But to answer your question, no, never any other time besides that one.
